## Editor’s Preface.

> Those of the golden race are capable of judging things most accurately and in every way.
> <note>original Greek: "Οἱ δὲ τοῦ χρυσοῦ γένυς κρίνειν ἀκριβέστατα καὶ πάντη εἰσὶν ἱκανοὶ." This quote is attributed to Plato, likely referencing his description of the "golden race" of guardians in the *Republic* who possess superior discernment.</note>
> ->*Plato*<-

> Those of the golden race are capable of judging things accurately and through and through.
> <note>The original text provided a German translation of the Greek quote above; it has been rendered here into English for consistency.</note>

With this prerequisite assumed, you will hopefully bow with as deep a reverence before the following little treatise as the author of the *Six Bowls* <note>The "Six Bowls" likely refers to a contemporary alchemical or satirical work, or perhaps a specific author known for a humble preface.</note> did before the public. Listen well to what Hermann Fictuld <note>Hermann Fictuld (1700–1777) was a prominent and prolific German author of alchemical and Rosicrucian literature, known for his "Hermetic Shield" and efforts to organize the Order of the Golden and Rosy Cross.</note> says: "The author of the Missive <note>This refers to the *Missiv an die hocherleuchtete Brüderschaft des Ordens der Gold- und Rosenkreuzer* (1710), a highly influential text in 18th-century secret societies.</note> has posed important questions that by right should have been answered for him, and no doubt this has happened; you beginners, however, should hold them in veneration." He has said little, but said much indeed, and is the only <term>philosopher<gloss>In alchemical literature, a 'philosopher' is an adept or 'philosopher of nature' who understands the hidden workings of the universe and the secrets of the Great Work.</gloss></term> who recommends this excellent writing. The others act as if it did not exist, let alone that anyone would have dared to respond to it.

<summary>In this preface, the editor introduces a small but significant alchemical treatise, citing the authority of Plato and the Rosicrucian writer Hermann Fictuld to demand respect for the work. The text suggests the work contains profound questions that most scholars have been too intimidated to address.</summary>

# Editor’s Preface.

At least to me, no answer is known. It <note>Referring to the treatise or the "Missive" mentioned on the previous page.</note> is rightly to be called the crown of all philosophers, and it alludes to the most secret and profound knowledge of nature, which is sought in many other writings in vain. According to the author’s own statement, the truths crowding within it concern the greatest work of <term>Hermeticism<gloss>The philosophical and alchemical tradition following the teachings of the legendary Hermes Trismegistus.</gloss></term>: namely, that it is easy, certain, effortless, and almost childlike <note>The phrase "child's play" (Latin: *ludus puerorum*) is a common alchemical motif suggesting that once the secret "agent" is found, the work proceeds with ease.</note>, whereas others, by contrast, are full of danger and difficulties. Certainly, he was as far from the smoke as the <term>coal-blowers<gloss>German: *Kohlenbläser*; a derogatory term used by true alchemists for those who only understood the physical fire and bellows—the "puffers"—rather than the spiritual or philosophical fire.</gloss></term> are from nature. You, of the golden race, will be in a position to judge it. There would still be time to answer the questions he presented to you, if they were to fit into your current system. *) He wrote

# Editor’s Preface.

it in the year 1710 and sent the manuscript by post to the then writing-addicted <note>German: *schreibsüchtigen*; implies the physician was a very prolific author.</note> city physician of Tännstädt in Thuringia, Christoph Hellwig, in order to have it printed; Hellwig indeed immediately had it printed at the front of the fourth part of his book: *Rare Medical, Anatomical, Chemical, Surgical, and Physical Cases and Observations* <note>original Latin: *Casus et Observationes medicinales, anatomicae, chymicae, chirurgicae, physicae, rariores*</note>, *Frankfurt and Leipzig, 1711*. Although Hellwig did not know him in person, he certainly knew him under a name other than **Polycarpus Chrysostomus**, *) (as he signed himself at the conclusion of his missive,) because they corresponded frequently and reported other curious things to one another. The second printing can be found in that exceedingly rare book: *The Cave of Nature and Art Revealed* <note>original Latin: *Antrum naturae et artis reclusum*</note>, *Nuremberg, 1710*, in octavo. The third is in *Johann Otto’s* <note>Likely referring to Johann Otto von Hellwig (1654–1698), a famous alchemist and physician.</note> »to occupy oneself with the <term>Mercurial root<gloss>A fundamental substance in alchemy, often identified as the "First Matter" or the essential spirit required for the Great Work.</gloss></term>, and through <term>coagulation<gloss>The process of thickening or solidifying a liquid substance.</gloss></term> or <term>calcination<gloss>The process of heating a substance to high temperatures to drive off volatile matter or achieve oxidation.</gloss></term> to increase the heavenly moisture; whoever does not understand this should refrain from entering the Order of our Rosicrucian Brotherhood, so that he is not cast out as a <term>Mamluk<gloss>In this context, a "Mamluk" (historically a slave-soldier) refers to a "renegade" or a false initiate who fails to grasp the true spiritual mysteries and is thus excluded from the brotherhood.</gloss></term>.« One should compare this with the *Compass of the Wise* and others.

*) Should this name not have a meaning relevant to the matter? I believe so. — The Editor. <note>The pseudonym "Polycarpus Chrysostomus" translates from Greek as "Many-fruited Golden-mouth," suggesting an author who is both prolific in wisdom and speaks of the "Golden" work of alchemy.</note>

# Editor’s Preface.

of Hellwig’s *Physical Curiosities* <note>original Latin: *curioſa phyſica*</note>, which Christoph Hellwig published for the second time in octavo <note>A book size where the sheet is folded three times to create eight leaves.</note> in the year 1714; and the 4th is a single, extremely rare edition in octavo without publisher, place of printing, or year. Besides these four printings, I know of no others, and they are, as already mentioned, very rarely encountered. Therefore, it will be a great pleasure to the enthusiasts and to those of the **golden race** <term>golden race<gloss>A poetic or alchemical term for the community of "True Philosophers" or alchemists seeking the perfection of nature and the soul.</gloss></term> that they are now being given a new, unadulterated printing compared against all four editions; just as they will read with pleasure the attached notes and the complete directory of 200 **Rosicrucian writings**, which I have compiled in the evening hours for my own amusement. Regard it as a contribution to **Fictuld’s Touchstone** <note>A reference to Hermann Fictuld’s *Probier-Stein* (1740), a famous and highly critical bibliography used to distinguish "true" alchemical works from "false" ones.</note>, or do with it what you will. It would be a pity, I thought, if all these collected reports were to be lost along with other superfluous papers cluttering my desk, since perhaps others might know how to make better use of them.

1783.

T. Y. R.

### Highly enlightened men, and secret friends of God. ###

Your revelation has long been awaited with painful yearning, although for a good while in vain, until finally one from your midst recently published a treatise <term>treatise<gloss>A formal written work dealing systematically with a subject; here, it refers to a specific alchemical or philosophical manuscript.</gloss></term> under a name that is hated by many, yet also loved by not all too *) few—namely, the Brotherhood from the Order of the Golden and Rosy Cross—wherein a complete preparation of the philo- <note>The word is hyphenated at the page break; it likely concludes as "philosophical stone" on the next page. The catchword "so" indicates the next page begins with "sophical."</note>

*) In one printing, "too" <note>original German: *zu*</note> is omitted.

sophical Stone *) is taught. I leafed through this book upon first encounter, read the preface with deliberation, and found that the writer must be not only a good and real Christian, but also a <term>central philosopher<gloss>In alchemical traditions, this refers to a philosopher who has reached the "center" or the fundamental truth of nature's operations.</gloss></term>; for besides the fact that he confesses that a <term>tincture<gloss>A potent alchemical liquid or medicinal preparation, often the "Elixir" used to transform metals or prolong life.</gloss></term> can be made from all metals—but especially from the root of gold—he also openly acknowledges that there is another, particularly easy, though very secret way to prepare the great philosophical mystery, which final work he [describes] in a special chapter of the

*) The full title is as follows: *The True and Perfect Preparation of the Philosophical Stone of the Brotherhood from the Order of the Golden and Rosy Cross, wherein the matter of this secret is called by its name, and the preparation from beginning to end is shown with all manual procedures; attached thereto are the laws or rules which the aforementioned Brotherhood keeps among themselves.* Published for the benefit of the sons of doctrine <note>original Latin: *filiis doctrinae*</note>, by S. R. Breslau 1710 and 1714, in octavo. With a cinnabar title <note>A title page printed in bright red ink, typical for prestigious or occult publications of the era.</note>. The editor, who called himself *Sincerus Renatus* <note>Latin for "Sincerely Reborn"</note> in the second edition of 1714, was actually Samuel Richter, a preacher at Hattmannsdorf near Landshut in Silesia. According to his own confession, he received the manuscript for copying from a devotee of the Art <note>A common historical term for the "Hermetic Art" or alchemy.</note> and subsequently had it printed. —The Editor.

## Missive

to coagulate <note>original: *coaguliren*; the process of solidifying a liquid</note> its own white body, \*) which I have done very often up to now, and have certainly always found it to be **exalted** \*\*) <note>original: *exaltiret*; refined to a higher state of potency</note> in both quality and quantity? 5) How to arrive at the **white tincture**, since the matter appears colored immediately upon every drying out of the water? 6) Whether and when a metallic ferment from common metals is to be added thereto? etc.

Regarding the **matter**, it lies hidden potentially in the **center of the world** <note>original Latin: *Centro mundi*</note>; it is at first invisible, but becomes visible through the air without any manual intervention, appearing as a vapor, and finally as a crystalline water. When the **Spirit of the Lord**—through which the world was created and man became a living soul—hovers upon this water, he warms it so that it becomes viscous and active, and then the **virgin earth** separates itself. This earth, which until now has been shone upon by neither Sun nor Moon, appears as a subtle, snow-white dust of Paradise, separating from the virgin water (which likewise comes down from above and has never yet touched the filth of the earth trodden by feet) and settles to the bottom.

This now, I believe, is our kabbalistic **naked Diana without clothing**, <note>original Latin: *nuda sine veste Diana*; in alchemy, Diana represents the moon or silver, and her "nakedness" refers to the pure, essential matter stripped of impurities</note> who in the virgin...

## The Great Mystery,
-> or <-
### The Magico-Cabalistic Secret

is nothing other than the Spirit of the Wisdom of God, brooding over the spiritual waters <note>original: *Ruach Chochmah-El, incubans aquis spiritualibus*. The phrase combines Hebrew terms for spirit and wisdom with a Latin description of the Creation from Genesis.</note>. This Spirit of Divine Wisdom appears in various ways. To me, who am but an untimely birth <note>A self-deprecating reference to the Apostle Paul in 1 Corinthians 15:8, suggesting the author feels unworthy or less advanced than other masters.</note>, and not worthy to be called a **Master** <term>Magus</term><note>A term for a high-level practitioner of natural philosophy and spiritual arts.</note>, it appeared in the following manner, and indeed in a single **Subject** <term>Subiecto</term><note>In alchemy, the "subject" is the base material or primary substance being worked upon.</note>; namely, in the **Miraculous Word** <note>original: *Verbo mirifico*</note> proceeding from simplicity, embodying itself, and transforming into various figures.

1) As a voice of a violent rushing wind.
2) As a vapor, smoke, and mist.
3) As pearly drops.
4) As a stream of living water, or water of life.
5) As a still, gentle whistling <note>An allusion to the "still small voice" in which God appeared to the prophet Elijah.</note>.
6) As a fire that takes hold of itself.
7) As a land in which milk, and consequently honey, flows.
8) As a trembling earth in the light.

10) As red wine.
11) As a hidden manna and food of angels.

And all of this is obvious, visible, tangible to the hands, feelable and touchable, yet kabbalistic <note>In this context, "kabbalistic" refers to the esoteric or hidden mystical meaning behind physical appearances.</note>. And this is what the Apostle is aiming at when he says in *Acts 17*, that we should seek the Lord, if we might perhaps feel and find Him, since He is not far from every one of us, for in Him we live, move, and have our being. This Spirit is called, among other things in *Wisdom 7* <note>Referring to the Wisdom of Solomon in the Apocrypha.</note>, a **Unified Spirit** (without clothing <note>original: *absque indumento*</note>), and yet also a **manifold spirit** (with clothing <note>original: *cum indumento*</note>); a breath of divine power, and a ray of the glory of the Almighty. Furthermore, our Matter, once it has now turned red <note>The "red" stage, or *Rubedo*, represents the final perfection of the alchemical process, where the substance attains its maximum power.</note>, has this wonderful property: that at certain times, especially around the equinoxes <note>original: *circa aequinoctia*</note>, it dissolves <note>original: *solviret*</note> of its own accord into a thick oil, and then of its own accord coagulates <note>original: *coaguliret*</note> and sets firm again. To speak briefly of the matter, I will demonstrate in an orderly fashion, according to all parts of the secret work <note>The "Great Work" (*Magnum Opus*) of alchemy.</note>, what the true nature of the situation is. See also the 4th Book of Esdras, Volume 1, Chapter 14, verse 39 <note>4 Esdras 14:39 describes a cup filled with a liquid like fire, which grants wisdom to the drinker.</note>, and let all the chapters be read through; likewise Chapter 6, verse 56. *)

<header>to the highly enlightened Brotherhood, etc.</header>
<sig>B 3</sig>

### (1.) Concerning the *Universal First Matter.* <note>original: *prima Materia Vniuersali*</note>

The *first matter* <note>The *prima materia* is the foundational, formless substance that alchemists believed was the starting point for creating the Philosopher's Stone.</note> is a divine mist, proceeding from God’s Paradise in the East, pouring itself into a great multitude of heavenly waters, and, through the hovering of the Spirit of God, curdling <note>The German *gerinnend* suggests a process like milk turning to cheese—a common metaphor for the solidification of spiritual substance into physical matter.</note> together into a snow-white earth.

### (2.) Concerning the Vessel.

For this work—since it is entirely divine and requires neither the laying on of hands nor any labor—one has no need of any vessel. For where would Adam in Paradise have had a glass or similar vessel, since at that time none had been made, nor was one necessary? Therefore, the Matter brings its own vessel with it,

---

Whether these sections stem from Christoph Hellwig <note>A 17th-century physician and prolific author of alchemical and medical texts.</note>, or another, or from the profound author himself, I do not wish to investigate now. So much is certain: that in the first edition of another’s work, Hellwig omitted the best passages, and even entire chapters, but in the second edition, he inserted and completed them again, as the chapter on salt in his brother’s *Curious Physics* <note>original: *Physica Curiosa*, a work by Johann Otto Hellwig.</note> sufficiently proves. — The Editor.

<summary>This page defines the Universal First Matter as a divine, biblical substance and argues that the alchemical process requires no man-made vessels, as the matter contains its own container. An editorial note discusses the bibliographical history of the text and the involvement of Christoph Hellwig.</summary>
<keywords>First Matter, Prima Materia, Paradise, Adam, Alchemical Vessel, Christoph Hellwig, Physica Curiosa, salt, creation</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the instructional list concerning the preparation of the First Matter. It concludes the section on the "Vessel" before moving on to the requirements of Time and the Furnace. The text includes cynical and scholarly annotations by the editor, questioning the authenticity of certain passages and mocking the reliance on physical equipment.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>
<header>Mission</header>

and has no need of any other for its *coagulation* <note>The process of a liquid thickening or solidifying into a solid mass; in alchemy, this is the "fixing" of the volatile spirit into a stable form.</note>: which is the *pure* truth. \*)

### (3.) Concerning Time.

An inexperienced person would have to spend a year and a day, indeed the duration \*\*) of his life, to *coagulate* this white earth without burning; an experienced person *accomplishes* <note>original: *prästiret*, from the Latin *praestare*.</note> this in a few days, a knowledgeable person in several hours, or even in an instant.

### (4.) Concerning the Furnace or *Athanor*.

Because the work is divine and not human, no furnace other than a divine one belongs to it; therefore, all artificial furnaces are far removed from it. \*\*\*)

<meta>catchword: (5.)</meta>

---

\*) These last four words were presumably inserted by Christoph Hellwig, because they are to be read only in the reprint found in the *Curious Physics* <note>original: *Curiosa Physica*, a 17th-century work on natural philosophy and alchemy.</note>. —The Editor.

\*\*) In the aforementioned reprint: The entire duration.

\*\*\*) The furnace-builders and coal-carriers want to live too! What are you thinking, my dear *Chrysostomus* <note>A name meaning "Golden-Mouthed," likely a pseudonym or a reference to the eloquence of the author.</note>? And would it not be a crying shame for the artificial furnaces, which stand next to each other like organ pipes at the gold-cookers' <note>A derisive term for "puffers"—alchemists who obsessed over the literal, physical melting of metals rather than the spiritual or philosophical art.</note> shops, if they were smashed to pieces? —The Editor.

<summary>This page concludes the requirements for the alchemical Great Work, asserting that true coagulation requires only the matter itself, a divine timeline, and a "divine furnace" rather than physical tools. The editor’s notes provide a skeptical look at the text’s authorship and mock the vanity of physical laboratory equipment.</summary>

B 4

### (5.) Concerning the Lute, or the Seal of Hermes.

Here I must remain silent, for I am not permitted to break the divine <term>Seal</term> <note>original: *Sigill*. In alchemy, the "Seal of Hermes" refers to the airtight sealing of a glass vessel, often by melting the neck closed (hermetic seal), but here it also refers to the vow of secrecy.</note>, nor to reveal it <term>indiscriminately</term> <note>original: *promiscue*.</note>: I sigh, therefore, with the wise man:

> Oh! that I could place a lock upon my mouth, and press a firm seal upon my lips, so that I might not fall thereby, nor sin by revealing this entire Art. <note>A paraphrase of a passage from the Wisdom of Sirach (Ecclesiasticus) 22:27.</note>

For whoever cannot remain silent will not be aided by the <term>Spirits of Hermes</term> <note>original: *Manes Hermetis*. The "Manes" are the ancestral spirits or shades of the deceased.</note>, <term>Pythagoras</term>, and <term>Harpocrates</term> <note>Harpocrates was the Greek god of silence, adapted from the Egyptian child-Horus.</note>, as they imposed <term>silence</term> <note>original: *silentium*.</note> so strictly upon their students. Therefore, let everyone take to heart this <term>Hermetic-Pythagorean-Harpocratic silence</term>, for otherwise he will terrify the good spirits with his chatter, causing them to flee from him when they see that he cannot keep the <term>mysteries</term> <note>original: *mysteria*.</note> secret.

### (6.) Concerning the Fire.

Because the work is divine, the fire must also be such, and not a single spark of artificial fire is added to the <term>Coagulation</term> <note>The process of solidifying or thickening a substance.</note>.

### (7.) Concerning the Colors.

The matter is initially coal-black; afterward it becomes sky-blue, (occasionally green).

green) *) then snow-white, finally golden-yellow, but lastly red like a ruby. <note>These colors correspond to the traditional alchemical stages: *viriditas* (greenness), *albedo* (whiteness), *citrinitas* (yellowness), and *rubedo* (redness).</note>

### (8.) Of its Form.

To speak of its <term>form</term> <note>original: *Gestalt*.</note>, it is a fatty, oily <term>essence</term> <note>original: *Wesen*.</note>, once it has become ruby-red, yet it is of a dry substance. It shines upon its surface like precious stones; it breathes out blue, golden-yellow-fiery, and snow-white-shining transparent parts, like <term>Marienglas</term> <note>original: *Marienglas*. A transparent mineral such as selenite or mica that can be split into thin plates, often used historically to cover religious images or in windows.</note>, and like pure, transparent gold, silver, and precious stones.

### (9.) Its Smell.

It is, on one hand, most foul, like that of a <term>carcass</term> <note>original: *Aas*. This refers to the "Putrefaction" stage, where the matter must "die" and decay before it can be purified.</note>; on the other hand, it is again very lovely and sweet, like marzipan.

### (10.) Its Taste.

It is sweet, and a tiny bit of it swallowed down instantly banishes all hunger and thirst.

## (11.) Its Virtues.

They are manifold; it yields gold, silver, mercury, etc. and precious stones; it is the sole medicine for the human and other bodies. In it alone and solely consists long life. It is that through which one recognizes all things, including the creation of the world; through it all spirits become known to us, and we to them, *) through it all wonders happen in heaven and on earth, through it one can speak with one's friend over a thousand and more miles **) without anyone else perceiving it, through it one can be several thousand fathoms <note>original: *Klafter*. A traditional unit of length, roughly 6 feet, used for measuring depth or distance.</note> under the earth in an instant, and yet in an instant be several million miles in heaven. Whoever will not believe such high wonders may leave it be, but let him only read Wisdom 6, 7, 8, 9, 10, 11 <note>original: *Sap.*, abbreviation for *Sapientia Salomonis* (The Wisdom of Solomon), a book of the Biblical Apocrypha often cited by alchemists to link the Stone with Divine Wisdom.</note>, and he will see what this <term>subject</term> <note>original: *Subjektum*. In alchemy, the "subject" refers to the raw material or the underlying substance of the Great Work.</note> is capable of.

## (12.) Its complete process is:

Take the One, cast away the number 2, and bring the Trinity through the Quaternary <note>original: *gevierte*. This refers to the "Axiom of Maria Prophetissa," a fundamental alchemical formula describing the transition from unity to complexity and back to a perfected unity.</note>

# Missive
<meta>catchword: The</meta>

I call this mist divine: 1) for the reason that the mist in the lower material world is the **first vehicle** <note>original: "primum vehiculum"</note> with which the **Creator Spirit** <note>original: "Spiritus opifex"</note>—which is explicitly called the Divine Spirit in Holy Scripture—is clothed. 2) Because this mist is the first matter upon which the Divine Spirit operates **directly** <note>original: "immediate"</note>, and thus deems it worthy of His influence. 3) Because Holy Scripture mostly represents the **Divine Presence** <note>original: "praesentiam diuinam"</note> or **Shekhinah** <term>Shekhinah: A Hebrew term referring to the dwelling or settled presence of God, often depicted as a cloud or light in the Tabernacle.</term> under the clothing of a mist. For so it stands in Sirach 24: Wisdom went out from the mouth of the Most High and covered the earth like a mist. <note>Compare Isaiah 6:4, 1 Kings 8:10–12, Exodus 40:34, 38, Chapter 20:21, etc.</note> *)

**Also**: The **Tohu and Bohu** <term>Tohu and Bohu: From the Hebrew phrase in Genesis 1:2 describing the earth as "formless and empty" or "chaos and waste."</term>—the darkness of the abyss (which thereafter **resolves** <note>original: "resolviret"</note> into water)—according to **Moses**. The dark swirling mist of **Jacob Böhme** <note>original: "I. B. Teutonici." This refers to the mystic Jacob Böhme, the "Teutonic Philosopher."</note> and **Theophrastus Paracelsus**.

The dark air of **Anaximenes** <note>Anaximenes of Miletus (c. 586–526 BC), a Pre-Socratic philosopher who proposed that air was the primary substance of the universe.</note>.

# To the Highly Enlightened Brotherhood, etc.

*That which is infinite, or the* \*) *first cause, works within creatures.*

By the Earth, however, [is meant] the **vessels receiving the influx of the divine idea** <note>original: vasa influxum ideae divinae recipientia</note>: namely, the fundamental forms of the creatures.

These now—\*\*) after they had partly turned their faces away and toward one another, and partly retracted their **rays** <note>original: radios</note>—became <term>Tohu wa-Bohu</term> <term>Tohu wa-Bohu: Hebrew for "without form and void," describing the chaotic state of the universe before the light was called forth.</term>, as already explained above. Moses, in Genesis 1, correctly describes their final state and how they began to unfold themselves—partly **together** <note>original: coniunctim</note>, partly **separately** <note>original: separatim</note>, according to their various dwellings and regions—through the hovering of the <term>Spirit of God</term> <note>original: Ruach Elohim</note>.

This **primordial matter** <note>original: prima materia</note> of the world is now, as has already been frequently stated, also the **primordial matter** of the Art [of alchemy]; and the **creative spirit** <note>original: spiritus opifex</note> of the world is also the **master craftsman** <note>original: artifex</note> of the <term>Great Work</term> <term>Great Work: Latin *magisterium*, the central goal of alchemy, aiming for the perfection of matter and the creation of the Philosopher's Stone.</term>, which is compared to an egg. 1) For this reason: because the Spirit of the Lord, as it were, broods over the matter through its hovering, just as a dove broods over her eggs. 2) Because the water [is compared] to the shell, the white earth to the egg-white, and the—

# Rosicrucian Writings.

Although I am neither a <term>Rosicrucian</term> <note>A member of the "Brotherhood of the Rose Cross," a secret society of mystics and alchemists that rose to prominence in the early 17th century.</note> nor a <term>Freemason</term>, but only a lover of <term>natural science</term> <note>original: "Naturwissenschaft"; in this 18th-century context, this refers to "Physica Sacra" or alchemical study of the natural world rather than modern biology or chemistry.</note>; nevertheless, I wish to place into your hands, my gentlemen of the **golden race** <note>A term of high respect for initiates, likely referencing the "Order of the Golden and Rosy Cross" or the spiritual "gold" of the enlightened soul.</note>, a complete directory of such writings, which you may place in your libraries. It would be desirable, says **Fictuld** in the *Probierstein* <note>Hermann Fictuld was a significant 18th-century writer on alchemy and a founder of the Order of the Golden and Rosy Cross. His work *Probierstein* (Touchstone), published in 1753, was a critical bibliography used to separate "true" alchemical works from "false" ones.</note>, that someone would have the inclination to publish anew all the writings of the Rosicrucian Brotherhood written from the year 1614 to 1630 <note>The "Rosicrucian Furor" began in 1614 with the publication of the *Fama Fraternitatis*. The period between 1614 and 1630 saw a massive outpouring of manifestos and responses across Europe.</note> together in one volume, because they are very useful and edifying. His wish is good, but has not yet been fulfilled, because they are very rarely encountered, known not even by their titles, and thus impossible to bring together. Some of the best-known have been reprinted for a few years now, and from these exactly is the least to be learned: for the true Rosicrucians have revealed the least, while the others, who were not true ones, discovered the most, as the **Au-** <note>The text cuts off mid-word; based on the catchword, the author is likely writing "Author" (Verfasser).</note>

I do not express myself too harshly when I bestow this name upon those who wish to make gold at the expense of others. I possess many of his manuscripts, which I found among my papers, and observe that his entire science of making gold consisted of prudence and industriousness. He could likely not express himself more clearly than when he writes: *Our material is spirit, not body.* <note>original Latin: "Nostra materia est spiritus non corpus." This is a fundamental alchemical axiom suggesting that the "Great Work" is a transformation of the human soul rather than physical metal.</note> But I do not wish to dwell long on his defense, lest I too be taken for a <term>Rosicrucian</term> <note>A member of a secret legendary brotherhood (the Order of the Rosy Cross) claiming specialized holy and scientific knowledge.</note>. Even the discoverer of the latest secrets seems to believe this.

he learned much from them that he otherwise would never have learned. —— After Rosenkreuz had grown weary of traveling, he went to England and lived not far from London (according to my papers), where he constructed an underground cave <note>This refers to the "Vault of Christian Rosenkreuz," a central myth in Rosicrucianism. It is often described as a symbolic repository of all human knowledge.</note> and wrote a book upon which he placed "G. L." instead of a title. He commonly had his late father’s brother’s son, Benedict Rosenkreuz, with him; Benedict was a very skilled painter and was therefore able to produce excellent sketches and drawings. He commanded Benedict, upon the taking of a great oath, that immediately after his death, he should seal the vault and place a certain large tablet before it upon which the names of his pupils were written, and that he should bury the entrance itself with earth. All of this was done with the greatest precision, so that nothing more was heard of Rosenkreuz.

The artistic clockwork <note>Referring to the mechanical automaton described on the previous page that guarded the tomb.</note> was presented to the King, and is still found in the royal Chamber of Art <term>Kunstkammer: a traditional cabinet of curiosities or repository for royal treasures and scientific marvels</term>. Above the tomb stood the words: "*After 120 years I shall open*" <note>original: "Post CXX. annos patebo"</note>. One also found the following epitaph on the wall: "*C. H. Ros. C., descended from the noble and splendid German R. C. Family; a man admitted to celestial and human mysteries and secrets through the divine revelations of his century, most subtle imaginations, and tireless labors; after he had guarded for posterity his more than royal or imperial treasure (which he had collected on his travels in Arabia and Africa), which was not yet suitable for his century, and had now established faithful and most closely joined heirs of his arts as well as his name, he fashioned a miniature world* <note>original: "mundum minutum," a reference to the concept of the Microcosm reflecting the Macrocosm</note> *responding in all its movements to that great world; and finally, having extracted this compendium of past, present, and future things, being over a century old, not by disease (which he had never experienced in his body, nor ever allowed to infest others...*"

This complete defense <term>apology</term> is, like all of Fludd’s works, exceedingly rare, especially because it was not reprinted along with those other works. (The collection of writings published under the name of the Rosicrucian brothers, which Gottfried Thomas of Troschenreut and Wiedersberg established, is such that you will scarcely find its like elsewhere <note>original Latin: "Collectio scriptorum, quae sub nomine fratrum Rosaecrucianorum edita sunt... talis est, qualem vix alibi inuenies"</note>.) In Borel’s *Chemical Library* <note>original Latin: "Borellii Biblioth. chem." 41) Henry Neuhaus's pious and most useful admonition regarding the Brothers of the Rose Cross, namely whether they exist, what they are like, etc. <note>original Latin: "Henrici Neuhusii pia et utilissima admonitio de Fratribus Roseae Crucis, nempe an sint, quales sint etc."</note> No location given, 1618 in octavo; and Frankfurt, 1621 in octavo. <note>"Octavo" refers to the book size, where the printer's sheet is folded into eight leaves.</note>

This is found in the *Catalog of rare books* collected by Pfundt, the pastor of Ummendorf; and the *Thomas Library*, Volume I, page 373, as well as Volume II, page 412. Also Borel's *Chemical Bibliography* <note>Pierre Borel (c. 1620–1671), author of "Bibliotheca Chimica."</note> page 67, and Fresnoy <note>Nicolas Lenglet du Fresnoy</note>, Volume III, page 286. From this Neuhaus we also have a book: *Basilian Aphorisms, or Hermetic Canons on the spirit, soul, and body of the greater and lesser world.* <note>original Latin: "Aphorismi Basiliani, seu canones hermetici de spiritu, anima et corpore medio maioris et minoris mundi." The "greater and lesser world" refers to the Macrocosm (the universe) and the Microcosm (man).</note> Marburg, 1614 in quarto, which is also contained within the *Chemical Theater* <note>original Latin: "Theatro chimico," a massive multi-volume collection of alchemical texts.</note>. There also exists a manuscript by one Albert Neuhaus (who is said to have been a physician and an Adept <note>In the alchemical tradition, an "Adept" is someone who has attained the highest level of knowledge and allegedly mastered the "Great Work."</note>), which had already lain hidden for over 105 years, but is now carefully preserved in Hanover by a connoisseur and enthusiast. It is possible that Henry Neuhaus was a relative of his.

In Rosicrucian and alchemical thought, the "Sixth Age" refers to a final period of enlightenment and spiritual renewal before the end of the world</note>, that is, the whole art and science of the highly enlightened Fraternity of Christian Rosenkreuß, Nuremberg 1617. *in octavo.* <margin># !</margin>

*Thomasian Library. Vol. I. pages 373. and 374. Fresnoy Volume III. page 283.*

120) **Response to the Illustrious Brothers of the Rosy Cross, 1618. *In octavo*.** <note>original: "Responsum ad Fratres Rosaceae Crucis illustres." An "octavo" refers to the book size, where a sheet is folded into eight leaves.</note>

Compare Friedrich Roth-Scholtz's *Chemical Theater*, Part 3. <note>original: "Rothscholtzens Theat. chem. 3ter Theil." Roth-Scholtz (1687–1736) was a prominent Nuremberg bookseller and publisher of alchemical and Rosicrucian texts.</note>

121) **Roger Bacon's Dedicatory Epistle to the Brothers of the Rosy Cross.** <note>original: "Rogerii Baconis Epistola dedicatoria Fratribus Rosae Crucis." Roger Bacon (c. 1214–1292) was a medieval philosopher; 17th-century writers often claimed him as a precursor to Rosicrucianism.</note>

In the *Chemical Theater*, Volume V, page 933. <note>original: "Theatri chemici." This refers to the *Theatrum Chemicum*, the most comprehensive compendium of alchemical writings in the Western world.</note>

122) **Cross without a Cross, by a certain Rosicrucian Author. Manuscript.** <note>original: "Crux absque Cruce, a quodam Authore Rosae Crucis." "MS" stands for manuscript, indicating it was a hand-written document rather than a printed book.</note>

In Pierre Borel’s *Chemical Library*, page 68. <note>original: "Borellii Biblioth. chem." Pierre Borel (c. 1620–1671) was a French scholar who produced the first significant bibliography of alchemy.</note>

123) **The Work and the Subsidiary Work of the Brothers of the Rosy Cross.** <note>original: "Ergon Parergon Fratr. R. C." These Greek terms refer to the "Primary Work" (the spiritual or alchemical Great Work) and the "Subsidiary Work" (secondary experiments or social deeds).</note>

In the cited Library, page 68. <note>Refers to Borel’s bibliography mentioned in the previous entry.</note>

124) **Restoration to the Fraternity of the Rosy Cross, 1619. *In octavo*.** <note>original: "Redintegratio an die Fraternität vom Rosenkreutz." *Redintegratio* implies a renewal or restoration of the order.</note>

125) **Response from Prague to the Fraternity, 1617. *In octavo*.** <note>original: "Responsio aus Prag an die Fraternität." Prague was a major center for occult and alchemical study under Emperor Rudolf II.</note>

In the cited collection.

140) Marcus Friedrich Rosenkreuzer, **Lower Astronomy**, or: **A Spagyric Review of the Seven Terrestrial Planets**: <note>original: "Astronomia inferior, oder: septem planetarum terrestrium spagyrica recensio." "Lower Astronomy" was a common term for alchemy, where the "terrestrial planets" refer to the seven metals (gold, silver, iron, etc.) that correspond to the celestial planets.</note> that is: Narrative and Mention of the Seven Earthly Planets, etc. Collected from many Hermetic writings, partly experimented upon with [the author's] own hand. Nuremberg, published by Christoph Endter, 1674. In octavo. <note><term>Spagyric</term> refers to alchemical medicine, produced by separating and then recombining the constituent parts of a substance to purify it.</note>

The "Rose Garden" (Rosarium) is a frequent title for alchemical collections.</note> Manuscript of 25 leaves.

### Missives.

There are so many of these missives that one can hardly distinguish the titles from one another.

140) Marci Friederich Rosenkreutzer, *Lower Astronomy*, <note>original: "Astronomia inferior"</note> or: *An Alchemical Review of the Seven Terrestrial Planets*: <note>original: "septem planetarum terrestrium spagyrica recensio." In alchemy, the "terrestrial planets" refer to the seven metals (Gold, Silver, Mercury, Copper, Iron, Tin, Lead) which were thought to be influenced by their celestial namesakes.</note> that is: An account and mention of the seven terrestrial planets, etc. Compiled from many hermetic writings, partly experimented upon by the author's own hand. Nuremberg, published by Christoph Endter, 1674. In octavo.

<margin>+</margin>
141) *Golden Rose or Rosary*, <note>original: "Rosa Aurea sive Rosarius"</note> a most excellent treatise on the philosopher's stone, <note>original: "de philosophorum lapide"</note> written by an anonymous but most learned philosopher. Manuscript of 25 leaves.

> See *Uffenbach Library Catalog* <note>original: "Bibliotheca Uffenbachiana," referring to the famous book collection of Zacharias Conrad von Uffenbach (1683–1734).</note> page 633. It must have appeared in print; for Friar Onuphrius de Marsciano <note>An 18th-century writer on alchemy and the occult.</note> cites this *Golden Rose* with the addition: *by the Brothers of the Rosy Cross*. <note>original: "a Fratribus Roseae Crucis."</note>

<margin>+ =</margin>
142) Simple A. B. C. Booklet, <note>This refers to a famous Rosicrucian instructional book, often containing complex symbolic diagrams intended for "children" or beginners in the faith.</note> that is, the teachings of the *Brothers of the Rosy Cross* <note>original: "fratrum Roseae Crucis"</note> for young students who diligently practice daily in the school of the Holy Spirit, depicted quite simply through images, for exercise or practice in the natural and theological light,

### by a Brother of the Rosy Cross. Manuscript
### in Folio. <note>A "folio" is a large book format where the paper sheets are folded only once, resulting in large pages, often used for prestigious or illustrated works.</note>

Friedrich Christian Ritter, a bookseller in Hamburg residing "Behind the Bleichen," <note>original: "hinter der Bleichen." This was a street in Hamburg near the Alster river, named for the fields where laundry was laid out to be bleached by the sun.</note> announced through an advertisement on January 18th, 1766, that he intended to engrave this most rare manuscript <note>original: "MSct."</note> on copper and print 100 copies by subscription <note>original: "Pränumeration." A common 18th-century practice where customers paid in advance to fund the expensive printing process.</note>. The price for enthusiasts was voluntarily set at 2 Louis d'or <note>A valuable French gold coin used across Europe at the time.</note>. For the engraving and printing, the most authentic and accurate original copy—more complete than any seen before—was to be used. Whether it was ever actually published is something that those of the "golden race" <note>original: "vom goldnen Geschlecht." This is an alchemical metaphor referring to the "Golden and Rosy Cross" order or to those who have attained spiritual and chemical perfection.</note> will know best; however, I will set down his short description of it here:

It consists of 40 illuminated copper plates along with the corresponding text, and the engravings themselves are arranged in the following order:

Fig. 1. The engraved title, a secret figure with the signatures of the Rosicrucians.

A term famously used by the mystic Jacob Boehme to describe the primordial state of God or the "mother" of all things.</note>, *Universal Study* <note>original: "studium vniversale."</note>, in the middle of which is a philosophical sphere; in its center, the portrait of the Savior is presented in an **oval figure**, holding the *Sign of the World* <note>original: "Signo Mundi." Also known as the Globus Cruciger, this is an orb topped by a cross representing Christ's authority over the earthly realm.</note> in his hand, carried by two angels. Below is the golden Order Cross of the **Rosicrucians**, surrounded by the virtues: Faith, Hope, Love, and Patience, all joined together with a ribbon to be worn as an insignia of the Order on the breast, along with many inscriptions.

Fig. 4. A secret figure concerning the beginning and the properties of the 4 elements.

### Fig. 40. A short discourse on philosophy and the highest being, in which the great secret <note>original: *Arkanum*. In alchemical and mystical traditions, an "arcanum" is a profound secret or a "mystery" of nature that is hidden from the uninitiated.</note> is presented.

Everyone can easily understand, Ritter continues, that I am not in a position to provide such a description of this miraculous book in all its dissected details, as one is accustomed to giving for other books, because this would require a deep insight into the mysteries described within. I have therefore sketched more of a shadow than the true image itself of this most rare manuscript. I consider it necessary to add only this one circumstance: that the figures, engraved in copper by the finest masters, are printed on royal paper <note>A high-quality, large-format paper used for prestigious publications.</note> with new type, and most cleanly illuminated <note>Colored by hand.</note> according to the colors of nature and hermetic philosophy <note>The philosophical and magical tradition attributed to Hermes Trismegistus, forming the basis of Western alchemy.</note>, and that the work is to be completed within one year.

Manuscript.

In *Johann Gottfried Meister’s* account of the transformation of base metals into better ones, one finds on page 83 a letter from *Frederico Gualdiano* (See *Fictuld’s Touchstone* <note>original: *Fictulds Probierstein*. This refers to a famous 1753 work by Hermann Fictuld, which served as a critical bibliography and guide for alchemists and Rosicrucians.</note> page 88), written in *Biberach* on September 15, 1721. In this letter, he recounts that he was sent by the Rosicrucians to *Biberach*, fifteen miles beyond *Nuremberg*, to request and bring back this manuscript—along with the two following ones—from a widow. Her husband had been an adept <note>A term for an alchemist who has successfully discovered the "Philosopher's Stone" or attained the highest level of chemical and spiritual mastery.</note>, but he had drowned in the River *Meuse* <note>original: *Maase*</note> in 1719 while in the company of other Rosicrucians. In a second letter from *Augsburg*, dated September 25, 1721, Gualdiano says he has not yet received the work, but hopes to get it into his hands soon, noting that it is such a rare work that it surpasses all other writings.

The sophist has engaged with Wisdom, who shows him all the errors in his works and, at the same time, places the true key to all operations into his hands without any reservation, both universally as well as particularly <note>original: *universaliter* and *particulariter*. A "universal" work refers to the creation of the grand elixir that cures all and transforms everything, while "particular" works are smaller-scale transformations of specific metals.</note>, so that such...

<meta>This page continues the detailed bibliography of alchemical and Rosicrucian manuscripts. <page-num>99</page-num>
<header>about Rosicrucian writings.</header>

...as the one I have read—which consists of 236 very finely written quarto pages <note>Quarto refers to a book size where each sheet of paper is folded twice to create four leaves, resulting in a medium-sized volume.</note>—sufficiently proves. The first part of this work, acting as the *Clavis*, or *Key of True Wisdom*, or *Dialogue of a Sophist with Wisdom* <note>A "sophist" in this alchemical context often refers to a person who possesses intellectual knowledge but lacks the true, spiritual, or practical "wisdom" of the adept.</note>, exists in various copies; however, these either deviate from one another or are incomplete, and they always lack the second and third parts.

In the *Medical and Alchemical Oracle* <note>original: *medicinisch und alchemistischen Oraculo*</note>, published in Ulm in 1772 and 1775 by the Stettin publishing house, a reprint of the first part can be read on page 38, which bears the following title:

147) *Secret of all Secrets, or Key of Wisdom of all philosophers and adepts* <note>original: *Clauis Sapientiae omnium philosophorum et adeptorum*</note> *in a golden jewel, and a philosophical compendium of truth* <note>original: *compendio veritatis philosophico*</note> *shared from an ancient manuscript from the year 1300. With 2 woodcut figures.*

This edition is the poorest and most incomplete, and the date and the woodcuts appear to me to be fraudulent <note>The author uses the term "untergeschoben," suggesting the 1300 AD date and the illustrations were faked or added by the publisher to make the book seem more ancient and valuable.</note>, as they are not to be found in the aforementioned complete manuscript.

to the *Chemical Heaven* <note>original Latin: *ad coelum chemicum*</note>, Amsterdam <note>original Latin: *Amstelaedami*</note>, 1687, he promised to publish a treatise under the title: *The Unlocked Chemical Heaven* <note>original Latin: *Coelum referatum chemicum*; "referatum" is likely a variation of "reseratum," meaning opened or unlocked</note>, and even repeated his promise at the end of the treatise. However, he later changed his mind and took it back in his *Wisdom Gone Mad* <note>original Latin: *Sapientia infaniente* (likely *insaniente*)</note>, in the same place <note>original Latin: *ibid.*</note>, 1689, Page 4. In general, the principles of **Toeltius** are as far removed from the principles of **Tollius** as common laboratory workers are from Nature herself. <note>The author is highlighting a distinction between Jacob Tollius, a Dutch classicist with alchemical interests, and "Toeltius," whose identity was often debated or conflated in alchemical circles. The "laborant" or laboratory worker was often a derogatory term for someone who practiced chemistry without understanding the philosophical or natural theory behind it.</note>

150) Ludovicus Conradus Orvius’s *Occult Philosophy* <note>original Latin: *occulta philosophia*</note>, or *The Heaven of the Wise and the Vexation of Fools* <note>original Latin: *Coelum sapientum et vexatio stultorum*</note>. In this work, it is shown—more orderly, clearly, and thoroughly than has been done by anyone else—how one should attain the acidic solvent <note>original German: *acidosischen Solventen*; refers to a potent chemical dissolver, often the "Universal Solvent" or "Alkahest" sought by alchemists</note> and true <term>hermetic science</term> <note>The study of alchemy and divine philosophy attributed to the mythical figure Hermes Trismegistus.</note>. At the same time, it contains a very curious account of the life of the author and a band of <term>Adepts</term> <note>Individuals believed to have successfully discovered the Philosopher's Stone.</note>. Published for the first time from a very old and rare manuscript for the enthusiasts by L. H. I. V. H. I- D. Printed in 1737.

# Regarding Rosicrucian Writings
H <note>This "H" is a signature mark, used by early printers to help bookbinders assemble the pages in the correct order.</note>

A saying from the Voss Anthology <note>The *Voßische Blumenlese* was a popular literary collection edited by Johann Heinrich Voss.</note>
> "Tubs thrown out for 
> whales" <note>original: "Ausgeworfene Tonnen für Wallfische." This is a metaphorical idiom suggesting a distraction or a decoy intended to divert someone’s attention from a more important or dangerous matter.</note>
seems to be the case here. The manuscript was sent to Leipzig for the sake of finding a publisher, but since no one there was willing to take it on, it could not be otherwise than that it was sent back again. Thereupon, according to some reports, it is said to have been revised anew, reworked, and that which might have been to the detriment of the Order was struck out and omitted—and so it was brought to print at the Montag publishing house in Regensburg. Be that as it may, immediately after publication, a multitude of letters were sent to Ringmacher in Berlin, all seeking an acquaintance with Mr. von Plumenoek, because he was thought to know the channel through which one could become acquainted with such a great man as Plumenoek was. His published *Compass of the Wise* <note>The *Compaß der Weisen* (1779) was a foundational and highly controversial text of the Gold- and Rosy-Cross, detailing their history and alchemical practices.</note> gave the greatest probability of this, though one could easily have imagined finding the Philosopher's Stone <term>Stein der Weisen: The legendary alchemical substance capable of turning base metals into gold and granting eternal life.</term> much sooner through thorough reading than by finding the whereabouts of Mr. von Plume-

170) **The Compass of the Wise** <note>original title: *Der Compaß der Weisen*</note>, described by a fellow member of the inner constitution of genuine and true Freemasonry; edited, with notes, a dedication, and a preface in which the history of this illustrious Order, from the beginning of its foundation, is clearly and faithfully presented, and the errors of several degenerate French Masonic lodges are uncovered, by **Ketmia Vere** <note>Ketmia Vere was a pseudonym used by Baron Hans Heinrich von Ecker und Eckhoffen (1750–1790).</note>. 172) **Epistle to the Sublime Unknown**, or the Genuine and Rightful Freemasons. 1781. In octavo. In commission by Christian Ludwig Stahlbaum in Berlin. 8 Groschen.
The author appears to be neither a Freemason nor a Rosicrucian, yet he demonstrates the most excellent and profound knowledge of nature; if any of the newer writings of this kind deserve to be read, it is certainly this one.

173) **The Very Latest Discovery of the Most Hidden Secrets** of the High Degrees of Freemasonry, or the True Rosicrucian; translated from the English, together with the Noachite <note>The "Noachite" or "Prussian Knight" refers to a specific high degree of Freemasonry—the 21st degree of the Ancient and Accepted Scottish Rite—based on the legend of the Tower of Babel and Noah.</note>, or Prussian Knight, as well as a letter from a Profane Person <term>Profane: In esoteric contexts, a person who has not been initiated into the secret mysteries or the Order.</term>. Esoteric and controversial books were often labeled as being printed in "Jerusalem," "Philadelphia," or "Cosmopolis" to evade censors or add an air of mystery.</note>, 1768. In octavo. 12 Groschen.
This work was reviewed in the *Compass of the Wise*. To this also belong:

174) **Second and Third Letters from a Profane Person** regarding the fortunate discovery of Freemasonry. 1768. In octavo.

<margin>+</margin> **By the same author:** *The Journey of Philaretus to Mount Mercury*. <note>original Latin: "Iter Philareti ad montem Mercurii." Philaretus was a common pseudonym in alchemical literature meaning "Lover of Virtue."</note>

This was sent by **Edmund Dickinson** <note>An English physician and chemist (1624–1707) who served King Charles II.</note> to **Theodor Mundan**, an adept <term>Adept: in alchemy, a person who has successfully discovered the "Philosopher's Stone" or achieved the highest level of chemical knowledge.</term> of forty years' standing, for his reading and to hear his judgment upon it. Mundan's response was that he had read this "philosophical novel" <note>The term "philosophical novel" here refers to an allegorical narrative used to convey alchemical secrets.</note> with great pleasure, and that it contained the most ingenious instructions. The translator of Mundan's letter in the first volume of **Schröder’s** *New Alchemical Library*, page 170, attributes this novel to **Noll** <note>Henricus Nollius.</note> in a note, and certainly not to Dickinson.

194) **Herrmann Bildz's** *Letter to the Reborn Brothers of the Rosy Cross*. <note>original Latin/German: "Schreiben an die wiedergebohrnen Brüder Rosaeae Crucis."</note> In octavo.

195) *The Received Confession of the Fraternity of the Rosy Cross* <note>original Latin: "Fraternitatis Rosatae Crucis confessio recepta."</note> by A. O. M. T. W., 1617. In octavo.

In quarto.

This was part of the rare Keck collection. <note>Likely referring to a specific private library or auction catalog of the time.</note>

198) *Martin Maximilian Pruggmayr, Doctor of Philosophy and Medicine: A Philosophical Examination of the True Elixir of Life, or the Genuine Philosophical Potable Gold, by which not only were all diseases of the human body once healed, but also the impure and leprous bodies of metals were cured. A work no less useful than necessary for all sons of the Hermetic art, etc. Salzburg, 1687. In octavo.* <note>Original Latin title: "Martini Maximiliani Pruggmayr... scrutinium philosophicum de vero elixire vitae, seu genuino auro potabili philosophico..."</note> With a copperplate engraving representing the entire work of the Rosicrucians.

I had almost forgotten this exceedingly rare book, which finally came into my hands at great expense. The possessors of the great secret of nature kept it hidden for so long, until finally the author of *The Compass of the Wise* <note>Referring to "Der Compass der Weisen," a major Rosicrucian text first published in 1779.</note> and his commentator were able to find it in their hearts—and were sincere enough—to recommend this primary textbook to true students. The former cites it on *pages* 216 and 217 regarding the knowledge of the raw material of the Stone <term>Stone: the Philosopher's Stone, the legendary alchemical substance capable of transmuting base metals and prolonging life.</term> and its various places of origin; he states that it describes the place of origin more candidly than the *Great and Little Farmer*. <note>Original: "der große und kleine Bauer," referring to alchemical works by Johann Grasshoff (c. 1560–1623).</note> Regarding the fire, they recommend the 93rd and 154th pages, and at another

The French title is: *The Count of Gabalis, or Conversations on the Secret Sciences.* <note>Original: "Le Comte de Gabalis ou entretiens sur les Sciences secretes."</note> Amsterdam, 1671. In duodecimo. Also Amsterdam, 1727. In quarto. <term>quarto: A medium-sized book format.</term> It is also cited in the appendix to the *Book of Constitutions of the Freemasons*. Frankfurt am Main, 1743. In octavo. Pages 122–124.

<header>Complete Catalog</header>

In the year 1731, at the Teubner bookstore then operating in Leipzig, a *Catalog of Rare Alchemical Manuscripts* <note>original: "Catalogus rariorum Manuscriptorum alchymicorum"</note> in quarto <term>quarto: a book size where the paper sheet is folded twice to create four leaves, resulting in a medium-sized volume</term> was made public, with the notice that such items were for sale there. Among these was the aforementioned manuscript, featuring the following description:
> “It contains
> “many theosophical, kabbalistic, ma-
> “gical, astronomical, and chemical
> “figures, all of which are very neat-
> “ly drawn and illuminated with ap-
> “propriate colors; alongside a
> “clear explanation, without parables,
> “and diagrams of the Philosopher’s Stone,
> “what it is, how it is to be prepared,
> “and how to obtain the first matter <note>original: "prima materia," the formless base of all matter required to create the Stone</note>, from which
> “the same is to be manufactured.
> “Composed by a Brother of the Rosy and
> “Golden Cross <note>original: "Fratre Rosae et Aureae Crucis"</note>, and therefore by
> “a true Adept <term>Adept: a person who has attained the highest level of knowledge in alchemy and has successfully created the Philosopher's Stone</term>, for his own
> “instruction, but which later
> “fell into foreign hands through a 
> “particular, fateful twist of providence.”

So ends the description. By all accounts, the late Pastor Pfund in Ummendorf—whose surviving library was auctioned in Helmstedt in the year 1779—had purchased it for himself: for one finds it in

<meta>catchword: his (dessen)</meta>

<summary>This page describes a rare Rosicrucian manuscript sold in Leipzig in 1731, which claimed to provide a clear, non-symbolic explanation of how to create the Philosopher's Stone. The text traces the provenance of this manuscript to the library of Pastor Pfund, which was auctioned in 1779.</summary>

--- Page 142 ---
<meta>This page continues the concluding remarks of the bibliography of Rosicrucian writings. The author is addressing the limitations of his catalog compared to larger existing collections and appealing to the "initiated" readers for their expertise.</meta>

<page-num>126</page-num>
<header>126 Complete List of Rosicrucian Writings.</header>

<meta>The paper shows signs of foxing and damp staining, particularly in the upper and left margins.</meta>

Writings which would constitute a library twice as large as that of Fresnoy <note>referring to Nicolas Lenglet Du Fresnoy (1674–1755), a French scholar whose extensive bibliography of hermetic and alchemical works was the standard reference of the 18th century</note> cannot, in any case, find room here. But you, of the **golden lineage** <note>original: "goldnen Geschlecht," a term used here to address the Rosicrucians themselves, implying they are a noble or spiritually enlightened class of people</note>, could now easily make corrections, insertions, and additions, if you were inclined to gift your secret information to the public; for the path has finally been cleared, though not without laborious research and compilation. Be satisfied with this work, and because only you are capable of judging it all, you will surely grant the editor a favorable judgment—or at least wish that the best of the listed old writings might be reprinted.

He expresses a hope that his bibliography will lead to the reprinting of important historical texts.</summary>
